m^{\left[k\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}=m^{\left[k\ell\right]}
.

Proof 1.46
.

We induct on
k
. The
base case
(
k=0
) is trivial (since
\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes 0}=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
and
0=0\ell
). For the
induction step
, we
fix a
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and we assume (as the induction hypothesis) that

m^{\left[k\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}=m^{\left[k\ell\right]}
. We must now prove that
m^{\left[k+1\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes\left(k+1\right)}=m^{\left[\left(k+1\right)\ell\right]}
. But
\left(k+1\right)\ell=k\ell+\ell
, and thus

\displaystyle m^{\left[\left(k+1\right)\ell\right]}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[k\ell+\ell\right]}=m\circ\left(\underbrace{m^{\left[k\ell\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=m^{\left[k\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\\
\text{(by the induction hypothesis)}\end{subarray}}\otimes\underbrace{m^{\left[\ell\right]}}_{=\operatorname*{id}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{eq.mu+v})}\right)

\displaystyle=m\circ\underbrace{\left(\left(m^{\left[k\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\right)\otimes\left(\operatorname*{id}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)\right)}_{=\left(m^{\left[k\right]}\otimes\operatorname*{id}\right)\circ\left(\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle=m\circ\left(m^{\left[k\right]}\otimes\underbrace{\operatorname*{id}}_{=m^{\left[1\right]}}\right)\circ\underbrace{\left(\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)}_{=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes\left(k+1\right)}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{m\circ\left(m^{\left[k\right]}\otimes m^{\left[1\right]}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=m^{\left[k+1\right]}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{eq.mu+v}))}\end{subarray}}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes\left(k+1\right)}=m^{\left[k+1\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes\left(k+1\right)}.

Thus,
m^{\left[k+1\right]}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes\left(k+1\right)}=m^{\left[\left(k+1\right)\ell\right]}

is proved, so that the induction is complete. This proves Lemma
1.45
.

Lemma 1.47
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}=\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}
.

Proof 1.48
.

Upon swapping
k
and
\ell
, this becomes the dual claim to Lemma

1.45
, so the same proof can be used (with all arrows reversed).

Lemma 1.49
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Define a permutation
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{k\ell}
as in Lemma
1.31
. Then,

\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}=\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}.

Proof 1.50
.

From
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 1.4.22(c)]
(applied to
k-1
and
\ell-1
instead
of
k
and
\ell
), we obtain

m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}=\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]},

(25)

where
m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}
is the map defined just as

m^{\left[\ell\right]}
but for the algebra
H^{\otimes k}
instead of

H
. However, it is easy to see (and should be known) that

m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta.

(26)

Indeed, this can be checked on pure tensors, using Lemma
1.31
.
7
7

7

Proof.
If
\left(h_{i,j}\right)_{i\in\left[\ell\right],\ j\in\left[k\right]}\in H^{\ell\times k}
is any

\ell\times k
-matrix over
H
, then

\displaystyle\left(\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta\right)\left(h_{1,1}\otimes h_{1,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1,k}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{2,1}\otimes h_{2,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{2,k}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ \cdots\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{\ell,1}\otimes h_{\ell,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,k}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\left(h_{1,1}\otimes h_{2,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,1}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{1,2}\otimes h_{2,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,2}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ \cdots\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{1,k}\otimes h_{2,k}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,k}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have applied the map }\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\\
\text{to both sides of the equality (\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.z-prod})}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{m^{\left[\ell\right]}\left(h_{1,1}\otimes h_{2,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,1}\right)}_{=h_{1,1}h_{2,1}\cdots h_{\ell,1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \otimes\underbrace{m^{\left[\ell\right]}\left(h_{1,2}\otimes h_{2,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,2}\right)}_{=h_{1,2}h_{2,2}\cdots h_{\ell,2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \otimes\cdots

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \otimes\underbrace{m^{\left[\ell\right]}\left(h_{1,k}\otimes h_{2,k}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,k}\right)}_{=h_{1,k}h_{2,k}\cdots h_{\ell,k}}

\displaystyle=h_{1,1}h_{2,1}\cdots h_{\ell,1}\otimes h_{1,2}h_{2,2}\cdots h_{\ell,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1,k}h_{2,k}\cdots h_{\ell,k}

\displaystyle=\left(h_{1,1}\otimes h_{1,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1,k}\right)\cdot\left(h_{2,1}\otimes h_{2,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{2,k}\right)\cdot\cdots\cdot\left(h_{\ell,1}\otimes h_{\ell,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,k}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}\left(h_{1,1}\otimes h_{1,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1,k}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{2,1}\otimes h_{2,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{2,k}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ \cdots\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\otimes\ h_{\ell,1}\otimes h_{\ell,2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell,k}\right).

In other words, the two maps
\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta
and
m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}
agree
on each pure tensor. Since these two maps are
\mathbf{k}
-linear, they must
therefore be identical (since the pure tensors span
H^{\otimes k\ell}
). In
other words,
\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta=m_{H^{\otimes k}}^{\left[\ell\right]}
. This proves
(
26
).

Using (
26
), we can rewrite (
25
)
as

\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}=\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}.

This proves Lemma
1.49
.

The next two lemmas combine the algebra and coalgebra structures with the grading:

Lemma 1.51
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Then,

P_{\gamma}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\gamma_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}.

Here,
\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)

denotes the
k\ell
-tuple consisting of all
k\ell
numbers
\gamma_{i,j}

(for all
i\in\left[k\right]
and
j\in\left[\ell\right]
) listed in
the order of lexicographically increasing pairs
\left(i,j\right)
.

Proof 1.52
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that
p_{i}:H\rightarrow H
denotes the
projection of the graded
\mathbf{k}
-module
H
onto its
i
-th graded
component. Likewise, let
p_{i}^{\prime}:H^{\otimes\ell}\rightarrow H^{\otimes\ell}
denote the projection of the graded
\mathbf{k}
-module

H^{\otimes\ell}
onto its
i
-th graded component. The map
m^{\left[\ell\right]}:H^{\otimes\ell}\rightarrow H
is graded
8
8

8

This follows
by a straightforward induction on
\ell
from the gradedness of the map

m:H\otimes H\rightarrow H
.
. Thus, it commutes with the projection onto the

i
-th graded component. In other words,

p_{i}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}=m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ p_{i}^{\prime}.

(27)

However, the definition of the grading on
H^{\otimes\ell}
yields that the

i
-th graded component of
H^{\otimes\ell}
is
\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=i\end{subarray}}H_{i_{1}}\otimes H_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes H_{i_{\ell}}
. Hence, it is easy to see that the projection
p_{i}^{\prime}
onto this
component is

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=i\end{subarray}}p_{i_{1}}\otimes p_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{i_{\ell}}.

In view of this, we can rewrite (
27
) as

\displaystyle p_{i}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=i\end{subarray}}p_{i_{1}}\otimes p_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{i_{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=i\end{subarray}}m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{i_{1}}\otimes p_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{i_{\ell}}\right)

(28)

(here, we have distributed the summation sign to the beginning of the product,
since all the maps involved are linear).

Forget that we fixed
i
. We have thus proved the equality (
28
)
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now, from
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)
,
we obtain
P_{\gamma}=p_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{k}}
(by the definition of
P_{\gamma}
). Hence,

\displaystyle\underbrace{P_{\gamma}}_{=p_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{k}}}\circ\underbrace{\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}}_{=m^{\left[\ell\right]}\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}\otimes\cdots\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}}

\displaystyle=\left(p_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{k}}\right)\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}\otimes\cdots\otimes m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p_{\gamma_{1}}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)\otimes\left(p_{\gamma_{2}}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)\otimes\cdots\otimes\left(p_{\gamma_{k}}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(p_{\gamma_{s}}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we use the symbol
}\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}f_{s}\text{ for }f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=\gamma_{s}\end{subarray}}m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{i_{1}}\otimes p_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{i_{\ell}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have applied
(\ref{pf.lem.Pm.3}) to }i=\gamma_{s}\text{ for each }s\in\left[k\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(\gamma_{s,1},\gamma_{s,2},\ldots,\gamma_{s,\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\gamma_{s,1}+\gamma_{s,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{s,\ell}=\gamma_{s}\end{subarray}}m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{\gamma_{s,1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,\ell}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have renamed the index }\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right)\\
\text{as }\left(\gamma_{s,1},\gamma_{s,2},\ldots,\gamma_{s,\ell}\right)\text{ in the sum}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{s,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{s,1}+\gamma_{s,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{s,\ell}=\gamma_{s}\text{ for all }s\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{\gamma_{s,1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,\ell}}\right)\right)

(31)

(by the product rule for tensor products, which says

\bigotimes\limits\limits_{s=1}^{k}\ \ \sum\limits_{a\in A_{s}}f_{s,a}=\sum\limits_{\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\right)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{k}}\ \ \bigotimes\limits\limits_{s=1}^{k}f_{s,a_{s}},

and which we here have applied to

A_{s}=\left\{\left(\gamma_{s,1},\gamma_{s,2},\ldots,\gamma_{s,\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\ \mid\ \gamma_{s,1}+\gamma_{s,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{s,\ell}=\gamma_{s}\right\}

and
f_{s,\left(\gamma_{s,1},\gamma_{s,2},\ldots,\gamma_{s,\ell}\right)}=m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{\gamma_{s,1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,\ell}}\right)
). However, if

\gamma_{s,j}
is a nonnegative integer for all
s\in\left[k\right]
and
all
j\in\left[\ell\right]
, then

\displaystyle\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ\left(p_{\gamma_{s,1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,\ell}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)}_{=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}}\circ\underbrace{\left(\bigotimes\limits_{s=1}^{k}\left(p_{\gamma_{s,1}}\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{\gamma_{s,\ell}}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}\\
\text{(by the definition of }P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}.

Thus, (
31
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle P_{\gamma}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{s,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{s,1}+\gamma_{s,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{s,\ell}=\gamma_{s}\text{ for all }s\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\gamma_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}.

This proves Lemma
1.51
.

Lemma 1.53
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Then,

\left(\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\gamma_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}.

Here,
\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)

denotes the
k\ell
-tuple consisting of all
k\ell
numbers
\gamma_{i,j}

(for all
i\in\left[k\right]
and
j\in\left[\ell\right]
) listed in
the order of lexicographically increasing pairs
\left(i,j\right)
.

Proof 1.54
.

This is the dual statement to Lemma
1.51
, and is proved by the same
argument “with all arrows reversed” (meaning that any composition
f_{1}\circ f_{2}\circ\cdots\circ f_{\ell}
of
several maps should be reversed to become
f_{\ell}\circ f_{\ell-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
) and with all
m
’s replaced by
\Delta
’s.

1.7
The composition formula: proof

We are finally able to prove Theorem
1.24
. The proof follows
the same paradigm as Patras’s proof of the commutative and cocommutative
particular cases (
[
Patras94
, preuve de Théorème II,7]
and

[
CarPat21
, proof of Theorem 5.11]
), but involves more complexity due to
the presence of permutations.

Proof 1.55

(Proof of Theorem
1.24
.)
.

The formula (
4
)
yields

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

(32)

\displaystyle p_{\beta,\tau}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ P_{\beta}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}.

(33)

Multiplying these two equalities (using the operation
\circ
), we obtain

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}=m^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}\circ P_{\beta}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}.

Our main task now is to “commute” the
operators in this equality past each other, moving both
m
factors to the
left, both
\Delta
factors to the right, and ideally obtaining some
expression of the form
m^{\left[k\ell\right]}\circ P_{\gamma}\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}
(more precisely, it will be a
sum of such expressions). For this purpose, we shall derive some
“commutation-like” formulas.

Lemma
1.51
(applied to
\alpha
and
\alpha_{i}
instead of
\gamma

and
\gamma_{i}
) yields

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}.

(34)

Lemma
1.53
(applied to
k
,
\ell
,
\beta
and
\beta_{i}
instead
of
\ell
,
k
,
\gamma
and
\gamma_{i}
) yields

\displaystyle\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ P_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[\ell\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[k\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,k}=\beta_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell,k}\right)}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{j,i}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[\ell\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[k\right];\\
\theta_{1,i}+\theta_{2,i}+\cdots+\theta_{k,i}=\beta_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have renamed
the }\gamma_{i,j}\text{ as }\theta_{j,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\text{ and }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have renamed
the indices }i\text{ and }j\text{ as }j\text{ and }i\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}

(35)

(here, we have just rewritten the “
\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}

for all
j\in\left[\ell\right]
and
i\in\left[k\right]
” under the summation sign as “
\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
i\in\left[k\right]
and
j\in\left[\ell\right]
”).

Next, we shall use a nearly trivial fact: If
f,g,u,v
are four maps such that
the compositions
f\circ u
and
g\circ v
are well-defined (i.e., the target
of
u
is the domain of
f
, and the target of
v
is the domain of
g
) and
satisfy
f\circ u=g\circ v
, and if the maps
f
and
v
are invertible, then

u\circ v^{-1}=f^{-1}\circ g

(36)

(since
\underbrace{f\circ u}_{=g\circ v}\circ\,v^{-1}=g\circ\underbrace{v\circ v^{-1}}_{=\operatorname*{id}}=g
and thus
g=f\circ u\circ v^{-1}
, so that
f^{-1}\circ g=\underbrace{f^{-1}\circ f}_{=\operatorname*{id}}\circ\,u\circ v^{-1}=u\circ v^{-1}
).

Define a permutation
\sigma^{\times\ell}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k\ell}
as in Lemma

1.33
. Define a permutation
\tau^{k\times}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k\ell}

as in Lemma
1.36
. Define a permutation
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{k\ell}
as in Lemma
1.31
.

Lemma
1.33
(applied to
f=m^{\left[\ell\right]}
) yields

\sigma\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\sigma^{\times\ell}
.
Hence, (
36
) (applied to
f=\sigma
and
u=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}
and
g=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}
and
v=\sigma^{\times\ell}
) yields

\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}=\sigma^{-1}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}.

(37)

Lemma
1.36
(applied to
f=\Delta^{\left[k\right]}
) yields

\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ\tau=\tau^{k\times}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}
. In other words,
\tau^{k\times}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}=\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ\tau
. Hence, (
36
) (applied to

f=\tau^{k\times}
and
u=\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}
and
g=\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}
and
v=\tau
) yields

\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ\tau^{-1}=\left(\tau^{k\times}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}.

(38)

Furthermore, if
\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}
is a nonnegative integer for each

i\in\left[k\right]
and
j\in\left[\ell\right]
, then we have

\displaystyle P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\zeta

\displaystyle=\zeta\circ P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)\cdot\zeta}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.Ppi}), applied to }\pi=\zeta\text{ and }\gamma=\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\zeta\circ P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}

(39)

(since Lemma
1.41
yields
\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)\cdot\zeta=\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)
) and

\displaystyle P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\sigma^{\times\ell}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\times\ell}\circ P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)\cdot\sigma^{\times\ell}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.Ppi}), applied to }\pi=\sigma^{\times\ell}\text{ and
}\gamma=\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\times\ell}\circ P_{\left(\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),1},\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),2},\ldots,\theta_{\sigma\left(k\right),\ell}\right)}

(since Lemma
1.43
yields

) and therefore

\sigma^{\times\ell}\circ P_{\left(\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),1},\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),2},\ldots,\theta_{\sigma\left(k\right),\ell}\right)}=P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\sigma^{\times\ell}
, so that

P_{\left(\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),1},\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),2},\ldots,\theta_{\sigma\left(k\right),\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}=\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}\circ P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{1,2},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}

(40)

(by (

and

and

).

Now, (

) and (

) yield

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\underbrace{\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\circ m^{\left[\ell\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\zeta\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.mDc})}\end{subarray}}\circ\,P_{\beta}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[\ell\right]}

\displaystyle=m^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha}\circ\underbrace{\sigma^{-1}\circ\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(m^{\left[\ell\right]}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.msig}))}\end{subarray}}\circ\,\zeta

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \circ\underbrace{\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ P_{\beta}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\

\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.Pm1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \circ\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ P_{\left(\theta_{1,1},\theta_{2,1},\ldots,\theta_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\underbrace{\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\circ\tau^{-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\tau^{k\times}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\otimes\ell}\\

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have distributed the summation sign to the}\\
\text{beginning of the product, since all our maps are }\mathbf{k}\text{-linear}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\

However, if

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \circ\underbrace{\left(\sigma^{\times\ell}\right)^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ\left(\tau^{k\times}\right)^{-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\tau\left[\sigma\right]\right)^{-1}\\

\displaystyle=m^{\left[k\ell\right]}\circ\begin{cases}P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)},&\text{if }\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)=\left(\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),1},\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),2},\ldots,\theta_{\sigma\left(k\right),\ell}\right);\\

}i,j;\\

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \circ\left(\tau\left[\sigma\right]\right)^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}

(since the condition “

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ m^{\left[k\ell\right]}\circ\begin{cases}P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)},&\text{if }\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all
}i,j;\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \circ\left(\tau\left[\sigma\right]\right)^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\

\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ m^{\left[k\ell\right]}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\tau\left[\sigma\right]\right)^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}

(here, we have discarded all the addends that do

satisfy

,
because these addends are

). Thus,

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \underbrace{m^{\left[k\ell\right]}\circ P_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}\circ\left(\tau\left[\sigma\right]\right)^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\ell\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.pas.formal-def}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\end{subarray}}p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}.

(46)

Now, let us simplify the inner sum in (
46
). If

\delta_{i,j}
and
\theta_{i,j}
are nonnegative integers for all

i\in\left[k\right]
and
j\in\left[\ell\right]
that satisfy
\left(\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\right)
,
then each
j\in\left[\ell\right]
satisfies

\displaystyle\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\sigma\left(1\right),j}+\theta_{\sigma\left(2\right),j}+\cdots+\theta_{\sigma\left(k\right),j}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\text{ is a bijection from }\left[k\right]\text{ to
}\left[k\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}

(since the condition
\left(\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\right)
allows us to rewrite each
\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}
as
\gamma_{i,j}
). Thus, the “
\theta_{1,j}+\theta_{2,j}+\cdots+\theta_{k,j}
” under the

) can

\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}
”. As a consequence, we can rewrite

) as

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\end{subarray}}p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right];\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all
}i,j\end{subarray}}p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}\\
\text{(since the condition \textquotedblleft}\gamma_{i,j}=\theta_{\sigma\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{ensures that }\theta_{i,j}=\gamma_{\sigma^{-1}\left(i\right),j}\text{ for all }i,j\text{,}\\
\text{and thus uniquely determines the }\theta_{i,j}\text{;}\\
\text{hence, the sum has exactly one addend)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}p_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}.

This proves Theorem
1.24
.

As we mentioned above, (
1
) is a particular case of
Theorem

using Proposition

(b)
. Likewise, the analogue of (

) for

.

Question 1.56
.

In
[
Pang21
, Proposition 12]
, Pang generalized
(
1
) to Hopf algebras with involutions (as long as
they are commutative or cocommutative). Is a similar generalization possible
for Theorem
1.24
?

1.8
Linear independence of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s

Both Theorem
1.24
and Proposition
1.19
hold for
arbitrary graded (not necessarily connected) bialgebras. Let us now focus our
view on the connected graded bialgebras (which, as we recall, are
automatically Hopf algebras).

Using Theorem
1.24
and (
7
), we can expand any
nested convolution-and-composition of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s as a
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of single
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s. Using Proposition

2.5
further below, we can furthermore transform the

\alpha
’s in the resulting combination into actual compositions, not just
weak compositions. This allows us to mechanically prove equalities for

p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s that involve only convolution, composition and

\mathbf{k}
-linear combination and that are supposed to be valid for any
connected graded Hopf algebra
H
. The reason why this works is the following
“generic linear independence” theorem:

Theorem 1.57
.

(a)
There is a connected graded Hopf algebra

H
such that the family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a composition of length }k\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

(of endomorphisms of
H
) is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent, and such that
each
H_{n}
is a free
\mathbf{k}
-module.

(b)
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, there is a connected graded Hopf
algebra
H
such that the family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a composition of length }k\text{ and size }<n\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

(of endomorphisms of
H
) is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent, and such that
each
H_{n}
is a free
\mathbf{k}
-module with a finite basis.

Proof 1.58

(Proof of Theorem
1.57
(sketched).)
.

(b)
Let
H
be
the free
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with generators

x_{i,j}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{with }i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ satisfying
}1\leq i<j\leq n.

We also set

x_{k,k}:=1_{H}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }k\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

We make the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
H
graded by declaring each
x_{i,j}
to be
homogeneous of degree
j-i
. We define a comultiplication
\Delta:H\rightarrow H\otimes H
on
H
to be the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism that satisfies

\Delta\left(x_{i,j}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=i}^{j}x_{i,k}\otimes x_{k,j}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ satisfying }1\leq i<j\leq n.

We define a counit
\epsilon:H\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
in the obvious way to
preserve the grading (so that
\epsilon\left(x_{i,j}\right)=0
whenever

i<j
). It is easy to see that
H
is a connected graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra, thus a connected graded Hopf algebra.
9
9

9

This
H
resembles
certain bialgebras that appear in the literature. In particular, it can be
regarded either as a noncommutative version of a reduced incidence algebra of
the chain poset with
n
elements, or as a noncommutative variant of the
subalgebra of the Schur algebra corresponding to the upper-triangular matrices
with equal numbers on the diagonal. But these connections are tangential for
our purpose.

It is easy to see that

\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\left(x_{i,j}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j}x_{u_{0},u_{1}}\otimes x_{u_{1},u_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}

for any
i\leq j
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for any
i\leq j
and any

k\in\mathbb{N}
and any permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)\left(x_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}\cdot\sum\limits_{i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j}x_{u_{0},u_{1}}\otimes x_{u_{1},u_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j}x_{u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}}\otimes x_{u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}}

and therefore (by applying the projection
P_{\alpha}
to both sides)

\displaystyle\left(P_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\right)\left(x_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j;\\
u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for each }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}x_{u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}}\otimes x_{u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}}.

Hence, for any composition
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and
any
1\leq i<j\leq n
satisfying
j-i=\left|\alpha\right|
, we have

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}\right)=x_{u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}}x_{u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}}\cdots x_{u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}},

where
\left(u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{k}\right)
is the unique strictly
increasing sequence of integers satisfying
u_{0}=i
and
u_{k}=j
and

u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}
for
all
i\in\left[k\right]

10
10

10

Why is there a unique sequence
with these properties? The easiest way to see this is as follows: Rewrite the
condition “
u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}
for all
i\in\left[k\right]
” as “
u_{j}-u_{j-1}=\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(j\right)}

for all
j\in\left[k\right]
” (here we just substituted

\sigma^{-1}\left(j\right)
for
i
). In the latter form, the condition
simply dictates the differences between consecutive entries of the sequence

\left(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\right)
. Thus, clearly, there exists a
unique sequence
\left(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\right)
of integers
satisfying this condition and having starting entry
u_{0}=i
. Moreover, this
sequence will be strictly increasing (since the differences
u_{j}-u_{j-1}=\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(j\right)}
are positive) and will
satisfy

\displaystyle u_{k}-u_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}\underbrace{\left(u_{j}-u_{j-1}\right)}_{=\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(j\right)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the telescope principle}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(j\right)}=\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(1\right)}+\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(2\right)}+\cdots+\alpha_{\sigma^{-1}\left(k\right)}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}=\left|\alpha\right|=j-i

and therefore
u_{k}=j-i+\underbrace{u_{0}}_{=i}=j-i+i=j
. Hence, it will be a
strictly increasing sequence
\left(u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{k}\right)
of
integers satisfying
u_{0}=i
and
u_{k}=j
and
u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}
for all
i\in\left[k\right]
.
. Hence, for any choice of
1\leq i<j\leq n
, the images
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}\right)
as
\alpha
runs over all compositions of
j-i
and

\sigma
runs over all permutations of
\left[\ell\left(\alpha\right)\right]
are distinct monomials
11
11

11

Why are they distinct? Because the
noncommutative monomial

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}\right)=x_{u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}}x_{u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}}\cdots x_{u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}}

allows us to uniquely reconstruct
\alpha
and
\sigma
as follows: Recall
that all the indeterminates
x_{p,q}
with
p<q
are distinct. The
noncommutative monomial
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}\right)=x_{u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}}x_{u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}}\cdots x_{u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}}
is a product
of
k
such indeterminates (since
u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{k}
, so that

u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}<u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}
for each
i
).
Hence, from this product, we can recover the pairs
\left(u_{\sigma\left(1\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(1\right)}\right),\ \left(u_{\sigma\left(2\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)}\right),\ \ldots,\ \left(u_{\sigma\left(k\right)-1},u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}\right)
, and
thus reconstruct the values
u_{\sigma\left(1\right)},u_{\sigma\left(2\right)},\ldots,u_{\sigma\left(k\right)}
. These, in turn, allow us to
reconstruct the permutation
\sigma
(since
u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{k}
).
Recalling the condition
u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}
once again, we then recover the
\alpha_{i}
and
thus the composition
\alpha
.
and therefore are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly
independent. This yields the
\mathbf{k}
-linear independence of the family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a composition of length }k\text{ and size }s\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

for any given
s\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
. Since each

p_{\alpha,\sigma}
lies in
\operatorname*{End}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}\left(H_{\left|\alpha\right|}\right)
, we thus obtain the
\mathbf{k}
-linear independence of the entire family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a composition of length }k\text{ and size }<n\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

(since the sum
\sum\limits_{s=0}^{n-1}\operatorname*{End}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}\left(H_{s}\right)
is a direct sum). This proves Theorem

1.57

(b)
.

(a)
As for part
(b)
, but remove “
\leq n
” everywhere.

Theorem
1.57
also has an analogue for graded bialgebras and
Hopf algebras without the connectedness requirement:

Theorem 1.59
.

(a)
There is a graded Hopf algebra
H
such
that the family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a weak composition of length }k\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

(of endomorphisms of
H
) is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent, and such that
each
H_{n}
is a free
\mathbf{k}
-module.

(b)
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, there is a graded Hopf algebra
H

such that the family

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}\text{;}\\
\alpha\text{ is a weak composition of length }k\text{
and size }<n\text{;}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\end{subarray}}

(of endomorphisms of
H
) is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent, and such that
each
H_{n}
is a free
\mathbf{k}
-module with a finite basis.

Proof 1.60

(Proof idea.)
.

(b)
This is similar to Theorem
1.57

(b)
, but there are more indices involved. We shall only give a rough
outline of the proof.

Let
H
be the free
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with generators

x_{i,j}^{p,q}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{with }\left(i,p\right),\left(j,q\right)\in\left[n\right]^{2}\text{ satisfying }i\leq j\text{ and
}p\leq q.

(The “
p,q
” superscript is not an
exponent!) Note that we don’t set any of the
x_{i,j}^{p,q}
equal to
1
,
unlike in the previous proof.

We make the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
H
graded by declaring each
x_{i,j}^{p,q}

to be homogeneous of degree
j-i
. We define a comultiplication
\Delta:H\rightarrow H\otimes H
on
H
to be the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
that satisfies

\Delta\left(x_{i,j}^{p,q}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=i}^{j}\ \ \sum\limits_{r=p}^{q}x_{i,k}^{p,r}\otimes x_{k,j}^{r,q}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all generators
}x_{i,j}^{p,q}.

We define a counit
\epsilon:H\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
by

\epsilon\left(x_{i,j}^{p,q}\right)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\left(i,p\right)=\left(j,q\right);\\
0,&\text{if }\left(i,p\right)\neq\left(j,q\right).\end{cases}

It is easy to see that
H
is a graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra. It is not
connected (with our grading) and not a Hopf algebra (since its grouplike
elements
x_{i,i}^{p,p}
have no inverses); we will say more about this later.

For any generator
x_{i,j}^{p,q}
of
H
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we can show
that

\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\left(x_{i,j}^{p,q}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j;\\
p=v_{0}\leq v_{1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{k}=q\end{subarray}}x_{u_{0},u_{1}}^{v_{0},v_{1}}\otimes x_{u_{1},u_{2}}^{v_{1},v_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}^{v_{k-1},v_{k}}.

Now, fix
i\leq j
in
\left[n\right]
. As we just showed, we have

\Delta^{\left[k\right]}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j;\\
1=v_{0}\leq v_{1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{k}=n\end{subarray}}x_{u_{0},u_{1}}^{v_{0},v_{1}}\otimes x_{u_{1},u_{2}}^{v_{1},v_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}^{v_{k-1},v_{k}}

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, by a similar argument as in the proof of
Theorem
1.57

(b)
, we can show that for any weak
composition
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and any
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
satisfying
j-i=\left|\alpha\right|
, we have

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}=j;\\
1=v_{0}\leq v_{1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{k}=n;\\
u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for each }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\left(x_{u_{0},u_{1}}^{v_{0},v_{1}}x_{u_{1},u_{2}}^{v_{1},v_{2}}\cdots x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}^{v_{k-1},v_{k}}\right)\leftharpoonup\sigma,

where the “
\leftharpoonup
” arrow denotes
the action of a permutation on the positions in a monomial (i.e., we set

\left(y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{k}\right)\leftharpoonup\sigma:=y_{\sigma\left(1\right)}y_{\sigma\left(2\right)}\cdots y_{\sigma\left(k\right)}

whenever
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and whenever
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{k}

are some of the generators of
H
).

We now claim that these images
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)
– where
k
ranges over
\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, where
\alpha

ranges over the weak compositions of length
k
and size
j-i
, and where

\sigma
ranges over
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
– are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly
independent. Unfortunately, they no longer are single monomials, but hope is
not lost: Each of them contains a monomial (with coefficient
1
) that is not
contained in any of the others (which ensures that any nontrivial
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of these images will have nonzero coefficients in front
of some of these monomials). To wit,
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)
contains (with coefficient
1
) the monomial

\left(x_{u_{0},u_{1}}^{1,2}x_{u_{1},u_{2}}^{2,3}\cdots x_{u_{k-1},u_{k-2}}^{k-1,k}x_{u_{k-1},u_{k}}^{k,n}\right)\leftharpoonup\sigma

for the unique weakly increasing sequence
\left(u_{0}\leq u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}\right)
of integers satisfying
u_{0}=i
and
u_{k}=j
and

u_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-u_{\sigma\left(i\right)-1}=\alpha_{i}
for
all
i\in\left[k\right]
(this monomial is obtained by taking
v_{i}=i+1

for each
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
v_{k}=n
), and this
monomial is not contained in any other
p_{\beta,\tau}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)
with
\left(\beta,\tau\right)\neq\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
(because reading its superscripts in order reveals the
permutation
\sigma
, and then undoing the
\sigma
-action and reading the
subscripts in order reveals the
u_{i}
’s and thus the weak composition

\alpha
). Thus, the images
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x_{i,j}^{1,n}\right)

are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent. Hence, so are the maps
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
themselves. From here, we can finish the proof of Theorem

1.59

(b)
just as in the proof of Theorem

1.57
, except that our
H
is just a bialgebra, not a Hopf algebra.

Now how do we make
H
into a Hopf algebra? The simplest way would be to
factor out the ideal generated by all differences of the form
x_{i,i}^{p,p}-1
; in other words, we replace the indeterminates
x_{i,i}^{p,p}
by
copies of
1
. With this change, our graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra is still
not connected; however, it would be connected with a different grading
(namely, with the grading in which each
x_{i,j}^{p,q}
is homogeneous of
degree
\left(j-i\right)+\left(q-p\right)
), and thus is a Hopf
algebra. The antipode is graded with respect to both gradings (this follows,
e.g., from
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 1.4.29(e)]
). Our above argument for the
linear independence of the
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s still applies with minor
changes, which we leave to the reader to perform.

(a)
As for part
(b)
, but remove “
\leq n
” everywhere and replace “
\left[n\right]
” by “
\mathbb{Z}
”.

1.9
The tensor product formula

We shall now connect the
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
operators on different graded
bialgebras. For this, we need a piece of notation:

Convention 1.1
.

The endomorphism
p_{\alpha,\sigma}:H\rightarrow H

defined for a given graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra
H
shall be denoted by
“
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
for
H
” when we want
to stress its dependence on
H
. Thus, for instance, if
K
is another graded

\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra, then the analogous endomorphism
p_{\alpha,\sigma}:K\rightarrow K
will be denoted by “
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
for

K
”.

Using this convention, we can now describe the endomorphism
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
for a tensor product of two
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebras:

Proposition 1.61
.

Let
H
and
G
be two graded bialgebras. Let

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
be a weak composition of length
k
, and let

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
be a permutation. Then,

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\text{ for }H\otimes G\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\text{;}\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(p_{\beta,\sigma}\text{ for }H\right)\otimes\left(p_{\gamma,\sigma}\text{ for
}G\right)

as endomorphisms of
H\otimes G
. Here,
\beta+\gamma
denotes the entrywise
sum of
\beta
and
\gamma
(that is, if
\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right)
and
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)
, then
\beta+\gamma:=\left(\beta_{1}+\gamma_{1},\ \beta_{2}+\gamma_{2},\ \ldots,\ \beta_{k}+\gamma_{k}\right)
).

Proof 1.62

(Proof sketch.)
.

We shall use the Sweedler notation again.

The definition of the grading on
H\otimes G
shows that
\left(H\otimes G\right)_{a}=\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\mathbb{N};\\
b+c=a\end{subarray}}H_{b}\otimes G_{c}
for any
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,

p_{a}\left(u\otimes v\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\mathbb{N};\\
b+c=a\end{subarray}}p_{b}\left(u\right)\otimes p_{c}\left(v\right)

(47)

for any
u\in H
, any
v\in G
and any
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

Write the weak composition
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
as
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
. Consider a pure tensor

h\otimes g\in H\otimes G
. Then, Remark
1.9
yields

\displaystyle p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(h\otimes g\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(h\otimes g\right)}p_{\alpha_{1}}\left(\left(h\otimes g\right)_{\left(\sigma\left(1\right)\right)}\right)p_{\alpha_{2}}\left(\left(h\otimes g\right)_{\left(\sigma\left(2\right)\right)}\right)\cdots p_{\alpha_{k}}\left(\left(h\otimes g\right)_{\left(\sigma\left(k\right)\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(h\otimes g\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\alpha_{i}}\left(\underbrace{\left(h\otimes g\right)_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}}_{=h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\otimes g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(h\right),\ \left(g\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\underbrace{p_{\alpha_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\otimes g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\mathbb{N};\\
b+c=\alpha_{i}\end{subarray}}p_{b}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\otimes p_{c}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.pas-on-tensors.grading}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(h\right),\ \left(g\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\mathbb{N};\\
b+c=\alpha_{i}\end{subarray}}p_{b}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\otimes p_{c}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(h\right),\ \left(g\right)}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\left(p_{\beta_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\otimes p_{\gamma_{i}}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the product rule}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for
all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all
}i\in\left[k\right]\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\text{ and}\\
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\left(h\right),\ \left(g\right)}\ \ \underbrace{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\left(p_{\beta_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\otimes p_{\gamma_{i}}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)}_{=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\beta_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)\otimes\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\gamma_{i}}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\text{ and}\\
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\left(h\right),\ \left(g\right)}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\beta_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)\otimes\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\gamma_{i}}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\text{ and}\\
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\left(h\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\beta_{i}}\left(h_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{\beta,\sigma}\left(h\right)\\
\text{(by Remark
\ref{rmk.pas.informal-def})}\end{subarray}}\otimes\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\left(g\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{\gamma_{i}}\left(g_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{\gamma,\sigma}\left(g\right)\\
\text{(by Remark \ref{rmk.pas.informal-def})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\text{ and}\\
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}p_{\beta,\sigma}\left(h\right)\otimes p_{\gamma,\sigma}\left(g\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}p_{\beta,\sigma}\left(h\right)\otimes p_{\gamma,\sigma}\left(g\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(p_{\beta,\sigma}\text{ for }H\right)\otimes\left(p_{\gamma,\sigma}\text{ for }G\right)\right)\left(h\otimes g\right).

Hence, the two
\mathbf{k}
-linear maps

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\text{ for }H\otimes G\right)\text{ and }\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k};\\
\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(p_{\beta,\sigma}\text{ for }H\right)\otimes\left(p_{\gamma,\sigma}\text{
for }G\right)

agree on each pure tensor. Therefore, they are identical. This proves
Proposition
1.61
.

1.10
The dual formula

Graded bialgebras often (not always) have duals. Indeed, if
H=\bigoplus\limits\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}H_{n}
is a graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra whose all
graded components
H_{n}
are finite free
12
12

12

“Finite
free” means “free of finite
rank”, i.e., “has a finite
basis”.

\mathbf{k}
-modules, then its graded dual

H^{o}:=\bigoplus\limits\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(H_{n}\right)^{\ast}
itself
becomes a graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra (see
[
GriRei20
, §1.6]
). Now we
claim (using Convention
1.1
again):

Proposition 1.63
.

Let
H
be a graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra whose all
graded components are finite free
\mathbf{k}
-modules. Consider its graded
dual
H^{o}
. Let
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
be a weak composition of length
k
, and let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
be a permutation. Then,

\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\text{ for }H^{o}\right)=\underbrace{\left(p_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1},\ \sigma^{-1}}\text{ for }H\right)^{\ast}}_{\text{restricted to }H^{o}}

as endomorphisms of
H^{o}
. Here, “
\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}
” is understood using the right action of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
on
\mathbb{N}^{k}
defined in (
8
).

Proof 1.64

(Proof sketch.)
.

By definition,
\left(p_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1},\ \sigma^{-1}}\text{ for }H\right)=m_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}\circ\underbrace{\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)^{-1}}_{=\sigma}\circ\,\Delta_{H}^{\left[k\right]}=m_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}\circ\sigma\circ\Delta_{H}^{\left[k\right]}
, so
that

\displaystyle\left(p_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1},\ \sigma^{-1}}\text{ for }H\right)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\left(m_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}\circ\sigma\circ\Delta_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\ast}\circ\sigma^{\ast}\circ\left(P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}\right)^{\ast}\circ\left(m_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\ast}.

(48)

However, the definition of the bialgebra structure on the graded dual
H^{o}

shows that
\left(\Delta_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\ast}=m_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}
and
\left(m_{H}^{\left[k\right]}\right)^{\ast}=\Delta_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}
. Moreover, basic linear algebra
(really just combinatorics of indices in tensor products) shows that

\sigma^{\ast}=\sigma^{-1}
and that
\left(P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}\right)^{\ast}=P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}
(since
\left(P_{\beta}\right)^{\ast}=P_{\beta}
for any weak composition
\beta
). Using these
four equalities, we can rewrite (
48
) as

\displaystyle\left(p_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1},\ \sigma^{-1}}\text{ for }H\right)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=m_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}\circ\underbrace{\sigma^{-1}\circ P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}\cdot\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)^{-1}}\circ\sigma^{-1}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.sol-mac-1.piP}))}\end{subarray}}\circ\,\Delta_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}

\displaystyle=m_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}\circ\underbrace{P_{\alpha\cdot\sigma^{-1}\cdot\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)^{-1}}}_{=P_{\alpha}}\circ\,\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}

\displaystyle=m_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}\circ P_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\Delta_{H^{o}}^{\left[k\right]}=\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\text{ for }H^{o}\right)

(again by the definition of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
). This proves Proposition

1.63
.

1.11
Have we found them all?

Now let
H
be a connected graded bialgebra. As we already mentioned, each

p_{\alpha,\sigma}
belongs to the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathbf{E}\left(H\right)
of all graded
\mathbf{k}
-module endomorphisms of
H
that
annihilate all but finitely many degrees of
H
. Thus, the same holds for any

\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of the
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
. The fact that each

p_{\alpha,\sigma}
annihilates all but the
\left|\alpha\right|
-th
graded component of
H
allows us to form infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear
combinations
\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}^{k}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\lambda_{\alpha,\sigma}p_{\alpha,\sigma}
of these
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
as well. Each such
combination belongs to
\operatorname*{End}\nolimits_{\operatorname*{gr}}H

(although not to
\mathbf{E}\left(H\right)
any more) and (since it is
natural in
H
) is therefore a natural graded
\mathbf{k}
-module endomorphism
of
H
defined for any connected graded bialgebra
H
.

Question 1.65
.

Are these combinations the only natural graded

\mathbf{k}
-module endomorphisms of
H
defined for any connected graded
bialgebra
H
?

In other words: Let
g
be a natural graded
\mathbf{k}
-module endomorphism
on the category of connected graded
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebras. (That is, for
each connected graded Hopf algebra
H
, we have a graded
\mathbf{k}
-module
endomorphism
g_{H}
, and each graded Hopf algebra morphism
\varphi:H\rightarrow H^{\prime}
gives a commutative diagram.) Is it true that
g
is
an infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s?

I have so far been unable to answer this question, for lack of convenient free
objects in the relevant category. Nevertheless, I suspect that the answer is
positive (i.e., every natural endomorphism is an infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear
combination of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s), at least when
\mathbf{k}
is a field
of characteristic
0
.

Question
1.65
can also be asked without the first
“graded”. That is, we can ask about natural

\mathbf{k}
-module endomorphisms of
H
that are not necessarily graded.

A similar question can be asked for general (as opposed to connected) graded
bialgebras. However, it requires some more care in its formulation, as not
every infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination
\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\lambda_{\alpha,\sigma}p_{\alpha,\sigma}
is well-defined (and we cannot
restrict the second sum to the
\alpha\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}^{k}
only).

2
The combinatorial Hopf algebra

\operatorname*{PNSym}

Let us now recall the connected graded Hopf algebra
\operatorname*{NSym}
of
noncommutative symmetric functions (introduced in
[
GKLLRT94
]
, recently
exposed in
[
GriRei20
, §5.4]
and
[
Meliot17
, Definition 6.1]
13
13

13

These three references give slightly different definitions of this
Hopf algebra
\operatorname*{NSym}
, but all these definitions are easily seen
to be equivalent (e.g., using
[
GKLLRT94
, Note 3.5, Proposition 3.8, Proposition
3.9]
and
[
Meliot17
, Proposition 6.2, Proposition 6.3]
). They also
denote it by different symbols: It is called
\mathbf{Sym}
in
[
GKLLRT94
]
,
called
\operatorname*{NSym}
in
[
GriRei20
, §5.4]
, and called

\operatorname*{NCSym}
in
[
Meliot17
, Definition 6.1]
(a name that means a
different Hopf algebra in most of the literature).
). As a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra, it is free with countably many generators
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{H}_{3},\ldots
, and its comultiplication is given
by
\Delta\left(\mathbf{H}_{m}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}\mathbf{H}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{H}_{m-i}
, where
\mathbf{H}_{0}:=1
. (We are using the
notation
\mathbf{H}_{k}
for the
k
-th complete homogeneous noncommutative
symmetric function in
\operatorname*{NSym}
, which is denoted
H_{k}
in

[
GriRei20
, Theorem 5.4.2]
, and denoted
S_{k}
in
[
GKLLRT94
, (22)]
and

[
Meliot17
]
.)

For any weak composition
\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, we set

\mathbf{H}_{\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\right)}:=\mathbf{H}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{H}_{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\mathbf{H}_{\alpha_{n}}\in\operatorname*{NSym}.

Thus, the family
\left(\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\text{ is a
composition}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{NSym}
.
(Note that
\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}
is called
S^{\alpha}
in
[
GKLLRT94
]
.)

An
internal product

\ast
is defined on
\operatorname*{NSym}
in

[
GKLLRT94
, §5.1]
. It is explicitly given by the formula

\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}\ast\mathbf{H}_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}\mathbf{H}_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right)}

for all compositions
\alpha
and
\beta
(see
[
GKLLRT94
, Proposition 5.1]
).
As mentioned in the introduction, Patras’s composition formula
(
1
) is structurally identical to this expression.
This shows that any cocommutative connected graded bialgebra
H
is a module
over the non-unital algebra
\operatorname*{NSym}\nolimits^{\left(2\right)}
of noncommutative symmetric functions equipped with its internal product.
(Each complete noncommutative symmetric function
\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}
acts as
the operator
p_{\alpha,\operatorname*{id}}
on
H
.)

It is natural to ask whether a similar construction can be made for any
connected graded bialgebra
H
using Theorem
1.24
. Thus, we are
looking for a non-unital algebra that contains elements
F_{\alpha,\sigma}

for all compositions
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and all permutations
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, and which acts on any connected graded bialgebra
H
by
having each
F_{\alpha,\sigma}
act as the operator
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
.

In this section, we shall construct such an algebra – which I name

\operatorname*{PNSym}\nolimits^{\left(2\right)}
for “permuted noncommutative symmetric functions”. Besides having
an “internal product”
\ast
, it has an
“external product”
\cdot
(corresponding
to the convolution of operators on
H
) and a coproduct
\Delta

(corresponding to acting on a tensor product of bialgebras).

2.1
Mopiscotions and weak mopiscotions

To construct this algebra, we need to make some implicit things explicit and
introduce some more notation:

Definition 2.1
.

A
mopiscotion
(short for “permuted
composition”) is a pair
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
,
where
\alpha
is a composition of length
k
(for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
) and

\sigma
is a permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
weak mopiscotion
is a pair
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
, where
\alpha
is a weak composition of length
k
(for
some
k\in\mathbb{N}
) and
\sigma
is a permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.

Clearly, any mopiscotion is a weak mopiscotion. In the reverse direction, we
can transform any weak mopiscotion
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
into a
mopiscotion
\left(\beta,\tau\right)
(non-injectively) by removing the
zeroes from the composition
\alpha
while appropriately reducing the
permutation
\sigma
as well. To give a precise definition, we need the
concept of
standardization
(
[
GriRei20
, Definition 5.3.3]
):

Definition 2.3
.

Let
w=\left(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{h}\right)
be any finite
list of integers (or of elements of any totally ordered set). Then, the

standardization
of
w
is defined as the unique permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{h}
with the property that for every two elements
a
and
b

of
\left[h\right]
satisfying
a<b
, we have the equivalence
\left(\sigma\left(a\right)<\sigma\left(b\right)\right)\Longleftrightarrow\left(w_{a}\leq w_{b}\right)
.

Roughly speaking, the standardization of the list
w
is the permutation whose
values are in the same relative order as the entries of
w
; when
w
has
equal entries, we count entries lying further left as being smaller. For
example, the two lists
\left(5,7,1,8,2\right)
and
\left(2,2,0,2,1\right)
have the same standardization, namely the permutation

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{5}
with one-line notation
\left[3,4,1,5,2\right]
. (In this paper, we will only use standardizations of lists that consist of
distinct entries. Thus, any subtleties related to equal entries can be
ignored, and we can simply compute the standardization of a list by replacing
its smallest entry by
1
, its second-smallest entry by
2
, and so on, and
finally read the resulting list as the one-line notation of a permutation.)

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
be a weak
mopiscotion, with
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. Let
\left(j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{h}\right)
be the list of all elements
i
of
\left[k\right]
satisfying
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
, in increasing order. Let
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{h}
be the standardization of the list
\left(\sigma\left(j_{1}\right),\sigma\left(j_{2}\right),\ldots,\sigma\left(j_{h}\right)\right)
. Let
\operatorname*{red}\alpha
denote the composition
\left(\alpha_{j_{1}},\alpha_{j_{2}},\ldots,\alpha_{j_{h}}\right)
(which consists
of all nonzero entries of
\alpha
). Then, we define
\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
to be the mopiscotion
\left(\operatorname*{red}\alpha,\tau\right)
. We call
\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
the
reduction
of
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
.

For example,

\operatorname*{red}\left(\left(3,0,1,2,0\right),\ \left[4,5,1,3,2\right]\right)=\left(\left(3,1,2\right),\ \left[3,1,2\right]\right),

where the square brackets indicate a permutation written in one-line notation.
For another example,

\operatorname*{red}\left(\left(3,0,1,2,0\right),\ \left[4,1,3,2,5\right]\right)=\left(\left(3,1,2\right),\ \left[3,2,1\right]\right).

Clearly, if
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
is a mopiscotion (i.e., if all
entries of
\alpha
are nonzero), then
\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)=\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
.

Mopiscotions have already appeared under the guise of “weighted permutations” in work by Foissy and Patras

[
FoiPat13
]
. Indeed, the set
\mathcal{S}
defined in
[
FoiPat13
, §3]
can be identified with the set of all mopiscotions, as long as we
translate each pair
\left(\sigma,d\right)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\operatorname*{Hom}\left(\left[k\right],\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{S}
(the notations used here are those of
[
FoiPat13
]
)
into the mopiscotion
\left(\delta,\sigma\right)
, where
\delta:=\left(d\left(1\right),d\left(2\right),\ldots,d\left(k\right)\right)
.
These weighted permutations
\left(\sigma,d\right)\in\mathcal{S}
are used
in
[
FoiPat13
]
to define certain linear endomorphisms of a shuffle Hopf
algebra, and might be generalizable to arbitrary graded bialgebras with a
dendriform structure, but do not agree with our maps
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
.

Let us now return to our maps
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
. If
H
is connected, we can
dispense with the ones in which
\alpha
contains zeroes, since there is a way
to reduce all such
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
to the ones where
\alpha
is a proper
composition (i.e., contains no zeroes):

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
H
be a connected graded bialgebra. Let
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
be a weak mopiscotion, and let
\left(\beta,\tau\right)=\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
. Then,

p_{\alpha,\sigma}=p_{\beta,\tau}.

Proof 2.6

(Proof idea.)
.

This is easy if one does not try to be rigorous. Here is a
handwavy proof using Sweedler notation:

The connectedness of
H
yields
p_{0}\left(h\right)=\epsilon\left(h\right)\cdot 1_{H}
for each
h\in H
. Hence, in the formula

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x\right)=\sum\limits_{\left(x\right)}p_{\alpha_{1}}\left(x_{\left(\sigma\left(1\right)\right)}\right)p_{\alpha_{2}}\left(x_{\left(\sigma\left(2\right)\right)}\right)\cdots p_{\alpha_{k}}\left(x_{\left(\sigma\left(k\right)\right)}\right)

(from Remark
1.9
), all the factors
p_{\alpha_{i}}\left(x_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)
with

\alpha_{i}=0
can be rewritten as
\epsilon\left(x_{\left(\sigma\left(i\right)\right)}\right)
(the
1_{H}
gets swallowed by the product) and
thus can be removed completely (using the
\sum\limits_{\left(h\right)}\epsilon\left(h_{\left(1\right)}\right)h_{\left(2\right)}=\sum\limits_{\left(h\right)}h_{\left(1\right)}\epsilon\left(h_{\left(2\right)}\right)=h
axiom of a coalgebra), as long as we remember to
adjust the permutation
\sigma
accordingly (removing its value
\sigma\left(i\right)
and decreasing all values larger than
\sigma\left(i\right)

by
1
). At the end of this process, we end up with
p_{\beta,\tau}\left(x\right)
. This shows that
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\left(x\right)=p_{\beta,\tau}\left(x\right)
for each
x\in H
. Proposition

2.5
follows.

A more rigorous version of this proof can be found in the Appendix (Section

A
).

2.2
\operatorname*{PNSym}

We can now define the combinatorial Hopf algebra that will occupy us for the
rest of this work:

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\operatorname*{PNSym}
be the free
\mathbf{k}
-module with basis
\left(F_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)\text{ is a mopiscotion}}
.

For any weak mopiscotion
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
, we set

F_{\alpha,\sigma}:=F_{\beta,\tau},

where
\left(\beta,\tau\right)=\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
.

Define two multiplications on
\operatorname*{PNSym}
: one “external multiplication” (which mirrors convolution of

p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s as expressed in Proposition
1.19
) given
by

F_{\alpha,\sigma}\cdot F_{\beta,\tau}=F_{\alpha\beta,\sigma\oplus\tau};

and another “internal multiplication” (which mirrors composition of
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
’s as expressed in Theorem

1.24
) given by

F_{\alpha,\sigma}\ast F_{\beta,\tau}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right]\text{ and }j\in\left[\ell\right];\\
\gamma_{i,1}+\gamma_{i,2}+\cdots+\gamma_{i,\ell}=\alpha_{i}\text{ for all }i\in\left[k\right];\\
\gamma_{1,j}+\gamma_{2,j}+\cdots+\gamma_{k,j}=\beta_{j}\text{ for all }j\in\left[\ell\right]\end{subarray}}F_{\left(\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{1,2},\ldots,\gamma_{k,\ell}\right),\tau\left[\sigma\right]}

(where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
). Also, we
define a comultiplication
\Delta:\operatorname*{PNSym}\rightarrow\operatorname*{PNSym}\otimes\operatorname*{PNSym}
on
\operatorname*{PNSym}

by

\Delta\left(F_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta,\gamma\text{
weak compositions;}\\
\text{entrywise sum }\beta+\gamma=\alpha\end{subarray}}F_{\beta,\sigma}\otimes F_{\gamma,\sigma}

(mirroring the formula from Proposition
1.61
).

We also equip the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{PNSym}
with a grading
by letting each
F_{\alpha,\sigma}
be homogeneous of degree
\left|\alpha\right|
.

These operations on
\operatorname*{PNSym}
behave as nicely as the analogous
operations on
\operatorname*{NSym}
:

Theorem 2.8
.

The
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{PNSym}
becomes a
connected graded cocommutative Hopf algebra when equipped with the external
multiplication
\cdot
, and a (non-graded) non-unital bialgebra when equipped
with the internal multiplication
\ast
. In particular, both multiplications
are associative.

There are two ways to prove this. I shall very briefly outline both:

Proof 2.9

(First proof idea for Theorem
2.8
.)
.

Most claims can be derived
from properties of the operators
p_{\alpha,\sigma}
, using the
H
from
Theorem
1.57

(a)
as a faithful representation.

For an example, let us prove that the internal multiplication
\ast
on

\operatorname*{PNSym}
is associative.

Let
H
be any connected graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra. Let

\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}:\operatorname*{PNSym}\rightarrow\operatorname*{End}H
be the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map that sends any

F_{\alpha,\sigma}
to the operator
p_{\alpha,\sigma}\in\operatorname*{End}H

for any mopiscotion
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
. Note that

\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\left(F_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)=p_{\alpha,\sigma}

(49)

is true not only for all mopiscotions
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
, but
also for all weak mopiscotions
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
(because if

\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
is any weak mopiscotion, and if
\left(\beta,\tau\right)=\operatorname*{red}\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
, then

p_{\alpha,\sigma}=p_{\beta,\tau}
and
F_{\alpha,\sigma}=F_{\beta,\tau}
).

Now, let
H
be the connected graded Hopf algebra
H
from Theorem

1.57

(a)
. Then, Theorem
1.57

(a)
says that the family
\left(p_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)_{\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)\text{ is a mopiscotion}}
is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly
independent. Hence, the linear map
\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}
is injective.

The formula for
F_{\alpha,\sigma}\ast F_{\beta,\tau}
that we used to define
the internal multiplication
\ast
is very similar to the formula for

p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}
in Theorem
1.24
. In
view of (
49
), this entails that

\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\left(F_{\alpha,\sigma}\ast F_{\beta,\tau}\right)=p_{\alpha,\sigma}\circ p_{\beta,\tau}=\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\left(F_{\alpha,\sigma}\right)\circ\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\left(F_{\beta,\tau}\right)

for any two mopiscotions
\left(\alpha,\sigma\right)
and
\left(\beta,\tau\right)
. By bilinearity, this entails that

\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\left(f\ast g\right)=\left(\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}f\right)\circ\left(\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}g\right)

for any
f,g\in\operatorname*{PNSym}
. Thus, the injective
\mathbf{k}
-linear
map
\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}:\operatorname*{PNSym}\rightarrow\operatorname*{End}H
embeds the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{PNSym}

with its binary operation
\ast
into the algebra
\operatorname*{End}H
with
its binary operation
\circ
. Since the latter operation
\circ
is
associative, it thus follows that the former operation
\ast
is associative
as well.

Similarly, we can show that the operation
\cdot
on
\operatorname*{PNSym}

is associative and unital with the unity
1=F_{\varnothing,\varnothing}

(although this is pretty obvious).

It is very easy to see that the cooperation
\Delta
is coassociative,
counital and cocommutative. It is also clear that both the multiplication

\cdot
and the comultiplication
\Delta
on
\operatorname*{PNSym}
are graded.

The next difficulty is to prove that
\Delta
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism, i.e., that
\Delta\left(fg\right)=\Delta\left(f\right)\cdot\Delta\left(g\right)
for all
f,g\in\operatorname*{PNSym}
(where
the “
\cdot
” on the right hand side is the
extension of the external multiplication
\cdot
to
\operatorname*{PNSym}\otimes\operatorname*{PNSym}
). Here, we can argue as above, using the fact (a
consequence of Theorem
1.61
) that

\displaystyle\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H\otimes H}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\otimes\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\right)\left(\Delta\left(f\right)\right)\in\operatorname*{End}\left(H\otimes H\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for every }f\in\operatorname*{PNSym}

to make sense of
\Delta
), and using the fact that the map

\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}\otimes\operatorname*{ev}\nolimits_{H}:\operatorname*{PNSym}\otimes\operatorname*{PNSym}\rightarrow\operatorname*{End}H\otimes\operatorname*{End}H\rightarrow\operatorname*{End}\left(H\otimes H\right)

is injective (this is not hard to show using the argument used in the proof of
Theorem
1.57
).

What we have shown so far yields that
\operatorname*{PNSym}
(equipped with

\cdot
and
\Delta
) is a connected graded
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra. Thus,
